Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/karpathy-guidelines.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Use when writing, reviewing, or refactoring code to avoid overcomplication, make surgical changes, surface assumptions, and define verifiable success criteria.
3: alwaysApply: true
4: ---
5: 
6: # Karpathy behavioral guidelines
7: 
8: Behavioral guidelines to reduce common LLM coding mistakes. Merge with project-specific instructions as needed.
9: 
10: **Tradeoff:** These guidelines bias toward caution over speed. For trivial tasks, use judgment.
11: 
12: ## 1. Think Before Coding
13: 
14: **Don't assume. Don't hide confusion. Surface tradeoffs.**
15: 
16: Before implementing:
17: 
18: - State your assumptions explicitly. If uncertain, ask.
19: - If multiple interpretations exist, present them - don't pick silently.
20: - If a simpler approach exists, say so. Push back when warranted.
21: - If something is unclear, stop. Name what's confusing. Ask.
22: 
23: ## 2. Simplicity First
24: 
25: **Minimum code that solves the problem. Nothing speculative.**
26: 
27: - No features beyond what was asked.
28: - No abstractions for single-use code.
29: - No "flexibility" or "configurability" that wasn't requested.
30: - No error handling for impossible scenarios.
31: - If you write 200 lines and it could be 50, rewrite it.
32: 
33: Ask yourself: "Would a senior engineer say this is overcomplicated?" If yes, simplify.
34: 
35: ## 3. Surgical Changes
36: 
37: **Touch only what you must. Clean up only your own mess.**
38: 
39: When editing existing code:
40: 
41: - Don't "improve" adjacent code, comments, or formatting.
42: - Don't refactor things that aren't broken.
43: - Match existing style, even if you'd do it differently.
44: - If you notice unrelated dead code, mention it - don't delete it.
45: 
46: When your changes create orphans:
47: 
48: - Remove imports/variables/functions that YOUR changes made unused.
49: - Don't remove pre-existing dead code unless asked.
50: 
51: The test: Every changed line should trace directly to the user's request.
52: 
53: ## 4. Goal-Driven Execution
54: 
55: **Define success criteria. Loop until verified.**
56: 
57: Transform tasks into verifiable goals:
58: 
59: - "Add validation" → "Write tests for invalid inputs, then make them pass"
60: - "Fix the bug" → "Write a test that reproduces it, then make it pass"
61: - "Refactor X" → "Ensure tests pass before and after"
62: 
63: For multi-step tasks, state a brief plan:
64: 
65: ```
66: 1. [Step] → verify: [check]
67: 2. [Step] → verify: [check]
68: 3. [Step] → verify: [check]
69: ```
70: 
71: Strong success criteria let you loop independently. Weak criteria ("make it work") require constant clarification.
72: 
73: ---
74: 
75: **These guidelines are working if:** fewer unnecessary changes in diffs, fewer rewrites due to overcomplication, and clarifying questions come before implementation rather than after mistakes.

(End of file - total 75 lines)
</content>

read · completed

Input

{
  "filePath": "/source/standards/ponytail.mdc"
}

Output

<path>/source/standards/ponytail.mdc</path>
<type>file</type>
<content>
1: ---
2: description: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.
3: globs:
4: alwaysApply: true
5: ---
6: 
7: # Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode
8: 
9: You are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.
10: 
11: Before writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:
12: 
13: 1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)
14: 2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.
15: 3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.
16: 4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.
17: 5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.
18: 6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.
19: 7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.
20: 
21: The ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.
22: 
23: Bug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.
24: 
25: Rules:
26: 
27: - No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.
28: - No new dependency if it can be avoided.
29: - No boilerplate nobody asked for.
30: - Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.
31: - Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.
32: - Question complex requests: "Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?"
33: - Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.
34: - Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.
35: 
36: Not lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.

(End of file - total 36 lines)
</content>

Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.\n\n(End of file - total 36 lines)\n</content>",

        "text": "---\ndescription: Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode. Always pick the simplest solution that works.\nglobs:\nalwaysApply: true\n---\n\n# Ponytail, lazy senior dev mode\n\nYou are a lazy senior developer. Lazy means efficient, not careless. The best code is the code never written.\n\nBefore writing any code, stop at the first rung that holds:\n\n1. Does this need to be built at all? (YAGNI)\n2. Does it already exist in this codebase? Reuse the helper, util, or pattern that's already here, don't re-write it.\n3. Does the standard library already do this? Use it.\n4. Does a native platform feature cover it? Use it.\n5. Does an already-installed dependency solve it? Use it.\n6. Can this be one line? Make it one line.\n7. Only then: write the minimum code that works.\n\nThe ladder runs after you understand the problem, not instead of it: read the task and the code it touches, trace the real flow end to end, then climb.\n\nBug fix = root cause, not symptom: a report names a symptom. Grep every caller of the function you touch and fix the shared function once — one guard there is a smaller diff than one per caller, and patching only the path the ticket names leaves a sibling caller still broken.\n\nRules:\n\n- No abstractions that weren't explicitly requested.\n- No new dependency if it can be avoided.\n- No boilerplate nobody asked for.\n- Deletion over addition. Boring over clever. Fewest files possible.\n- Shortest working diff wins, but only once you understand the problem. The smallest change in the wrong place isn't lazy, it's a second bug.\n- Question complex requests: \"Do you actually need X, or does Y cover it?\"\n- Pick the edge-case-correct option when two stdlib approaches are the same size, lazy means less code, not the flimsier algorithm.\n- Mark deliberate simplifications that cut a real corner with a known ceiling (global lock, O(n²) scan, naive heuristic) with a `ponytail:` comment naming the ceiling and upgrade path.\n\nNot lazy about: understanding the problem (read it fully and trace the real flow before picking a rung, a small diff you don't understand is just laziness dressed up as efficiency), input validation at trust boundaries, error handling that prevents data loss, security, accessibility, the calibration real hardware needs (the platform is never the spec ideal, a clock drifts, a sensor reads off), anything explicitly requested. Lazy code without its check is unfinished: non-trivial logic leaves ONE runnable check behind, the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an assert-based demo/self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures). Trivial one-liners need no test.",

623: // preDestroyNodes runs the PreDestroy stage of the given nodes concurrently, before any of them
624: // is deleted. Nodes without a container are skipped. A workers value of 0 runs all hooks at once.
625: func (*CLab) preDestroyNodes(ctx context.Context, nodes []clabnodes.Node, workers uint) {
626: 	if workers == 0 || workers > uint(len(nodes)) {
627: 		workers = uint(len(nodes))
628: 	}
629: 
630: 	sem := make(chan struct{}, workers)
631: 	wg := new(sync.WaitGroup)
632: 
633: 	for _, n := range nodes {
634: 		wg.Add(1)
635: 		sem <- struct{}{}
636: 
637: 		go func() {
638: 			defer wg.Done()
639: 			defer func() { <-sem }()
640: 
641: 			if n.GetContainerStatus(ctx) == clabruntime.NotFound {
642: 				return
643: 			}
644: 
645: 			if err := n.PreDestroy(ctx); err != nil {
646: 				log.Warnf("node %q pre-destroy hook failed: %v", n.Config().ShortName, err)
647: 			}
648: 		}()
649: 	}
650: 
651: 	wg.Wait()
652: }
653: 
654: func (c *CLab) deleteNodes(ctx context.Context, workers uint) {
655: 	wg := new(sync.WaitGroup)
656: 
657: 	concurrentChan := make(chan clabnodes.Node)
658: 
659: 	workerFunc := func(i uint, input chan clabnodes.Node, wg *sync.WaitGroup) {
660: 		defer wg.Done()
661: 
662: 		for {
663: 			select {
664: 			case n := <-input:
665: 				if n == nil {
666: 					log.Debugf("Worker %d terminating...", i)
667: 					return
668: 				}
669: 
670: 				err := n.Delete(ctx)
671: 				if err != nil {
672: 					log.Errorf("could not remove container %q: %v", n.Config().LongName, err)
673: 				}
674: 			case <-ctx.Done():
675: 				return
676: 			}
677: 		}
678: 	}
679: 
680: 	// start concurrent workers
681: 	wg.Add(int(workers))
682: 
683: 	for i := range workers {
684: 		go workerFunc(i, concurrentChan, wg)
685: 	}
686: 
687: 	// send nodes to workers
688: 	for _, n := range c.Nodes {
689: 		concurrentChan <- n
690: 	}
691: 
692: 	// close channel to terminate the workers
693: 	close(concurrentChan)
694: 
695: 	// also call delete on the special nodes
696: 	for _, n := range c.getSpecialLinkNodes() {
697: 		err := n.Delete(ctx)
698: 		if err != nil {
699: 			log.Warn(err)
700: 		}
701: 	}
702: 
703: 	wg.Wait()
704: }
705: 
706: // deleteContainersDirect deletes containers directly via the runtime API.
707: // This is used when we don't have node objects (e.g., destroy-by-name-only case).
708: func (c *CLab) deleteContainersDirect(
709: 	ctx context.Context,
710: 	containers []clabruntime.GenericContainer,
711: ) {
712: 	for _, cont := range containers {
713: 		if len(cont.Names) == 0 {
714: 			log.Warnf("skipping container %s with no names", cont.ID)
715: 			continue
716: 		}
717: 		name := cont.Names[0]
718: 		log.Infof("Removing container: %s", name)
719: 
720: 		c.logoutTailscaleContainer(ctx, cont)
721: 		rt := cont.Runtime
722: 		if rt == nil {
723: 			rt = c.globalRuntime()
724: 		}
725: 		err := rt.DeleteContainer(ctx, name)
726: 		if err != nil {
727: 			log.Errorf("could not remove container %q: %v", name, err)
728: 		}
729: 
730: 		_ = clabutils.DeleteNetnsSymlink(name)
731: 	}
732: }
733: 
734: func (c *CLab) deleteToolContainers(ctx context.Context) {
735: 	toolTypes := []string{"sshx", "gotty"}
736: 
737: 	if len(c.nodeFilter) == 0 {
738: 		toolTypes = append(toolTypes, clabnodestailscale.ToolType)
739: 	}
740: 
741: 	for _, toolType := range toolTypes {
742: 		toolFilter := []*clabtypes.GenericFilter{
743: 			{
744: 				FilterType: "label",
745: 				Field:      clabconstants.ToolType,
746: 				Operator:   "=",
747: 				Match:      toolType,
748: 			},
749: 			{
750: 				FilterType: "label",
751: 				Field:      clabconstants.Containerlab,
752: 				Operator:   "=",
753: 				Match:      c.Config.Name,
754: 			},
755: 		}
756: 
757: 		containers, err := c.globalRuntime().ListContainers(ctx, toolFilter)
758: 		if err != nil {
759: 			log.Error("Failed to list tool containers", "tool", toolType, "error", err)
760: 			continue
761: 		}
762: 
763: 		if len(containers) == 0 {
764: 			log.Debug("No tool containers found for lab", "tool", toolType, "lab", c.Config.Name)
765: 			continue
766: 		}
767: 
768: 		log.Info("Found tool containers associated with a lab", "tool", toolType, "lab",
769: 			c.Config.Name, "count", len(containers))
770: 
771: 		for idx := range containers {
772: 			containerName := strings.TrimPrefix(containers[idx].Names[0], "/")
773: 
774: 			log.Info("Removing tool container", "tool", toolType, "container", containerName)
775: 
776: 			c.logoutTailscaleContainer(ctx, containers[idx])
777: 			if err := c.globalRuntime().DeleteContainer(ctx, containerName); err != nil {
778: 				log.Error("Failed to remove tool container", "tool", toolType,
779: 					"container", containerName, "error", err)
780: 			} else {
781: 				log.Info(
782: 					"Tool container removed successfully",
783: 					"tool",
784: 					toolType,
785: 					"container",
786: 					containerName,
787: 				)
788: 			}
789: 		}
790: 	}
791: }

793: func cliPromptToDestroyAll(topos map[string]string) error {
794: 	var sb strings.Builder
795: 
796: 	idx := 1
797:

800: 		idx++
801: 	}

808: 		" to confirm or ENTER to abort: "
809: 	fmt.Print(warningStyle.Render(prompt))
810:

812:

816: 	}
817: 
818: 	answer := strings.TrimSpace(input)
819: 	if answer == "" {
820: 		return errors.New("aborted by the user. No labs were removed")
821: 	}
822: 
823: 	answer = strings.ToLower(answer)
824: 	if answer != "y" && answer != "yes" {
825: 		return errors.New("aborted by the user. No labs were removed")
826: 	}
827: 
828: 	return nil

42: // BridgeByName returns a *netlink.Bridge referenced by its name.
43: func BridgeByName(name string) (*netlink.Bridge, error) {
44: 	l, err := netlink.LinkByName(name)
45: 	if err != nil {
46: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("could not lookup %q: %v", name, err)
47: 	}
48: 	br, ok := l.(*netlink.Bridge)
49: 	if !ok {
50: 		return nil, fmt.Errorf("%q already exists but is not a bridge", name)
51: 	}
52: 	return br, nil
53: }
54: 
55: // LinkContainerNS creates a symlink for containers network namespace
56: // so that it can be managed by iproute2 utility.
57: func LinkContainerNS(nspath, containerName string) error {
58: 	CreateDirectory("/run/netns/", clabconstants.PermissionsDirDefault)
59: 	dst := "/run/netns/" + containerName
60: 	if _, err := os.Lstat(dst); err == nil {
61: 		os.Remove(dst)
62: 	}
63: 	err := os.Symlink(nspath, dst)
64: 	if err != nil {
65: 		return err
66: 	}
67: 	return nil
68: }
69: 
70: // GenMac generates a random MAC address for a given OUI.
71: func GenMac(oui string) (net.HardwareAddr, error) {
72: 	buf := make([]byte, 3)
73: 	_, _ = rand.Read(buf)
74: 
75: 	hwa, err := net.ParseMAC(fmt.Sprintf("%s:%02x:%02x:%02x", oui, buf[0], buf[1], buf[2]))
76: 	return hwa, err
77: }
78: 
79: // DeleteNetnsSymlink deletes a network namespace and removes the symlink created by LinkContainerNS
80: // func.
81: func DeleteNetnsSymlink(n string) error {
82: 	log.Debug("Deleting netns symlink: ", n)
83: 	sl := fmt.Sprintf("/run/netns/%s", n)
84: 	err := os.Remove(sl)
85: 	if err != nil {
86: 		log.Debug("Failed to delete netns symlink by path:", sl)
87: 	}
88: 	return nil
89: }

91: // ParkingNetnsName returns the deterministic named-netns identifier used to park

Reasoning
Now let me understand the context. `deleteContainersDirect` is used when destroying by name only (no topo file). It deletes containers via runtime API directly, without node objects, so `node.DeleteNetnsSymlink()` is not called. The PR adds `_ = clabutils.DeleteNetnsSymlink(name)` there to clean up netns symlinks.

Concerns:
1. `deleteContainersDirect` deletes ALL containers matching the filter — which could include tool containers (sshx, gotty, tailscale)? Let me check what filter is used and where deleteContainersDirect is called from. Tool containers are handled separately by `deleteToolContainers`. Let me check the call sites and the filter.

2. The container name may have leading "/" prefix (docker runtime Names[0] typically starts with "/"). In `deleteToolContainers` they TrimPrefix the name. In `deleteContainersDirect`, `name := cont.Names[0]` is passed to `rt.DeleteContainer(ctx, name)` and now to `DeleteNetnsSymlink(name)`. If Names[0] is "/clab-mgmt13-n1", then the symlink path would be "/run/netns//clab-mgmt13-n1" which resolves to "/run/netns/clab-mgmt13-n1" — actually double slash is fine, path resolves the same. os.Remove("/run/netns//clab-mgmt13-n1") is equivalent to os.Remove("/run/netns/clab-mgmt13-n1"). So double slash is fine on Linux.

But wait — what does `LinkContainerNS` use as containerName when creating the symlink? Let me check. It probably uses LongName like "clab-mgmt13-n1". Let me check where LinkContainerNS is called.

3. The test: "Netns symlinks are created for both nodes" — checks `/run/netns/clab-mgmt13-n1`. The node names presumably are n1, n2 with lab prefix mgmt13, so container long name is clab-mgmt13-n1. OK.

4. Error handling: `_ = DeleteNetnsSymlink(name)` — the function itself never returns an error (always returns nil), so ignoring is consistent with line 616 in the same file. Fine.

5. Filter concern: does deleteContainersDirect get called with filters that include tool containers? If yes, deleting the symlink for a tool container is harmless-ish but the name may not exist. Not a big deal since DeleteNetnsSymlink logs debug and returns nil.

Let me check the call sites of deleteContainersDirect and what filters are used.

528: 		return networks
529: 	}

531: 		return networks
532: 	}

535: 	for _, m := range networks {
536: 		defined[m.Network] = true
537: 	}

539: 		if defined[name] {
540: 			continue
541: 		}
542: 		defined[name] = true
543: 		// ExternalAccess mirrors the default every management network gets, so
544: 		// the forwarding rule installed for the network is cleaned up too.
545: 		networks = append(networks, &clabtypes.MgmtNet{

547: 			ExternalAccess: new(true),
548: 		})
549: 	}

Reasoning
Now I understand the flow:

- `destroy()` with nodes → `deleteNodes` (node.Delete which handles netns symlink via node.DeleteNetnsSymlink at line 467... wait actually line 465-471 iterates over `c.Nodes` and calls `node.DeleteNetnsSymlink()` after deletion).
- Actually, wait: line 616 `_ = clabutils.DeleteNetnsSymlink(ctr.Names[0])` in `deleteApplyNodes` — that's in the apply path.
- destroy-by-name-only (`len(c.Nodes)==0`) → `deleteContainersDirect(ctx, containers)` — previously did NOT remove netns symlinks. Now it does via the added line 730.

So the PR fixes a leak: destroy-by-name (--name without topo) leaves /run/netns/ symlinks behind. That's the bug the test covers.

Potential issues to consider:

1. **Name format mismatch**: `cont.Names[0]` from docker runtime typically includes a leading "/". Does `DeleteNetnsSymlink` handle that? Path becomes `/run/netns//clab-mgmt13-n1` — on Linux, `//` is treated as `/`, so `os.Remove` still works. Actually let me verify what GenericContainer.Names look like in this codebase's docker runtime. In deleteToolContainers, they do `strings.TrimPrefix(containers[idx].Names[0], "/")` — implying Names[0] may have leading slash. But in deleteContainersDirect pre-existing code, `rt.DeleteContainer(ctx, name)` is called with the raw name and it works (docker API accepts both). For the symlink path, `/run/netns//name` still resolves correctly on Linux. So not a real bug, but worth checking. Actually wait — is it? `fmt.Sprintf("/run/netns/%s", name)` with name="/clab-x" gives "/run/netns//clab-x". Linux collapses multiple slashes, so os.Remove works. Fine.

But in the node-based path (line 465-471), `node.DeleteNetnsSymlink()` uses `d.OverwriteNode.GetContainerName()` which returns LongName without slash. So both paths end up removing the same file. Consistent.

Hmm, but wait: does the node-based path call `DeleteNetnsSymlink` too, resulting in double-delete? For destroy with nodes, line 444 calls `deleteContainersDirect(ctx, orphanSidecars)` — orphan sidecars are tailscale containers not in c.Nodes. For those, deleting their symlink is appropriate (though LinkContainerNS is only called for regular nodes during startup... let me check). Actually LinkContainerNS is called in runtime/docker/docker.go:1421 — likely in `CreateContainer` or `StartContainer` for all nodes. Tool containers (sshx/gotty/tailscale) are created via a different path maybe. Let me check whether orphan sidecars would have had netns symlinks. If they didn't, `DeleteNetnsSymlink` fails silently (logs debug, returns nil). Harmless.

2. **Test concern**: The test "Netns symlinks are removed for both nodes" uses `Command Should Fail    test -L ...`. Does the destroy-by-name path run before this test? Yes, test case order is sequential. But one concern: after `destroy --name mgmt13 --cleanup`, other tests in the suite... no, this is a standalone suite.

Another concern: the netns symlink creation during deploy — where does that happen? `runtime/docker/docker.go:1421` — check that it uses node.LongName = "clab-mgmt13-n1". Let me look.

3. **Concurrency**: `deleteContainersDirect` is called sequentially in destroy-by-name; no issue. In the node-based path, it's called after `deleteNodes`, for orphan sidecars only. OK.

10: // ListOption is a type used for functional options for the Clab List method.
11: type ListOption func(o *ListOptions)
12: 
13: // ListOptions represents the options for listing containers.
14: type ListOptions struct {
15: 	containerName   string
16: 	labName         string
17: 	nodeName        string
18: 	toolType        string
19: 	clabLabelExists bool
20: 	cliArgs         []string
21: 	filters         []*clabtypes.GenericFilter
22: }
23: 
24: // NewListOptions returns a new list options object.
25: func NewListOptions() *ListOptions {
26: 	return &ListOptions{}
27: }
28: 
29: // ToFilters converts the list options to a slice of generic filters.
30: func (o *ListOptions) ToFilters() []*clabtypes.GenericFilter {
31: 	var filters []*clabtypes.GenericFilter
32: 
33: 	if o.containerName != "" {
34: 		filters = append(
35: 			filters,
36: 			&clabtypes.GenericFilter{
37: 				FilterType: "name",
38: 				Match:      o.containerName,
39: 			},
40: 		)
41: 	}
42:

44: 		filters = append(

50: 				Match:      o.labName,
51: 			},
52: 		)

63: 			},
64: 		)
65: 	}
66:

68: 		filters = append(
69: 			filters,
70: 			&clabtypes.GenericFilter{
71: 				FilterType: "label",
72: 				Field:      clabconstants.ToolType,
73: 				Operator:   "=",
74: 				Match:      o.toolType,
75: 			},
76: 		)
77: 	}
78:

80: 		filters = append(

85: 				Operator:   "exists",
86: 			},

Reasoning
So in destroy-by-name-only mode, `ListContainers(WithListLabName(...))` lists ALL containers labeled with the lab name — this includes tool containers (sshx/gotty/tailscale sidecars) since they carry the `containerlab` label. So `deleteContainersDirect` already deletes tool containers, then `deleteToolContainers` redundantly attempts to delete them again (pre-existing behavior). The new `DeleteNetnsSymlink(name)` call will also run for tool containers. Tool containers don't get netns symlinks created (LinkContainerNS is only called in postStartActions for node containers via CreateContainer... wait, actually do tool containers go through CreateContainer? sshx/gotty/tailscale are created as tool containers, presumably via runtime CreateContainer too? Let me check how sshx containers are created. Actually the tailscale test at tailscale_test.go:692 calls deleteContainersDirect with a container and checks... let me look at that test to see if it asserts on symlink behavior.

Reasoning
OK. The test for deleteContainersDirect passes containers with Names like "clab-mylab-ts" (no leading slash). DeleteNetnsSymlink will call os.Remove("/run/netns/clab-mylab-ts") — it might fail if the file doesn't exist (logs debug, returns nil). Fine in tests.

Now, some deeper considerations:

1. **Podman runtime Names format**: In the podman runtime, Names[0] — check whether it includes a leading "/". Docker's ContainerList returns names with leading "/". The existing code at line 717 `name := cont.Names[0]` and passes to `rt.DeleteContainer` — works either way. For the symlink removal, `/run/netns//clab-x` works on Linux (POSIX collapses multiple slashes). Let me check docker runtime's ListContainers to see how Names is populated.

Actually let me check how docker.go builds GenericContainer Names.

445: }
446: 
447: // produceGenericContainerList takes a list of containers in a podman entities.ListContainer format
448: // and transforms it into a GenericContainer type.
449: func (r *PodmanRuntime) produceGenericContainerList(ctx context.Context,
450: 	cList []entities.ListContainer,
451: ) ([]runtime.GenericContainer, error) {
452: 	genericList := make([]runtime.GenericContainer, len(cList))
453: 	for i, v := range cList {
454: 		netSettings, err := r.extractMgmtIP(ctx, v.ID)
455: 		if err != nil {
456: 			return nil, err
457: 		}
458: 		genericList[i] = runtime.GenericContainer{
459: 			Names:           v.Names,
460: 			ID:              v.ID,
461: 			ShortID:         v.ID[:12],
462: 			Image:           v.Image,
463: 			State:           v.State,
464: 			Status:          v.Status,
465: 			Labels:          v.Labels,
466: 			Pid:             v.Pid,
467: 			NetworkSettings: netSettings,
468: 			Ports:           []*types.GenericPortBinding{},
469: 		}
470: 
471: 		// Extract network name from labels
472: 		if netName, ok := v.Labels["clab-net-mgmt"]; ok && netName != "" {
473: 			genericList[i].NetworkName = netName
474: 		} else {
475: 			genericList[i].NetworkName = "unknown"
476: 		}
477: 
478: 		// convert the exposed ports the GenericPorts and add them to the GenericContainer
479: 		for _, p := range cList[i].Ports {
480: 			genericList[i].Ports = append(genericList[i].Ports,
481: 				netTypesPortMappingToGenericPortBinding(p)...)
482: 		}
483: 
484: 		genericList[i].SetRuntime(r)
485: 	}
486: 	log.Debugf("Method produceGenericContainerList returns %+v", genericList)
487: 	return genericList, nil
488: }

step-start
{
  "type": "step-start",
  "id": "prt_1773427cc001JvzktQzjhbpiEQ",
  "sessionID": "ses_e88cd3eb7ffetoKovjjzQ8Lxb0",
  "messageID": "msg_1773422a3001ynINUhABh0SuHK"
}
Reasoning
Docker trims leading "/" so Names[0] = "clab-mgmt13-n1". Podman `v.Names` — podman's list returns names without leading slash typically. Fine.

Now, another consideration: node-based destroy path. When nodes exist, `deleteContainersDirect` is called only with `orphanSidecars` (tailscale containers not in nodes). For these, `DeleteNetnsSymlink` will attempt to remove `/run/netns/clab-<lab>-<parent>-ts` — those sidecars are also created via runtime CreateContainer with postStartActions? Let me check how tailscale sidecars are created. Actually line 616 in deleteApplyNodes already does this same thing for recreated nodes, so the pattern is established. Whether the sidecar ever had a symlink is irrelevant — os.Remove fails silently.

But wait — there's a subtlety in the node-based path: after `deleteNodes`, `destroy()` iterates `c.Nodes` and calls `node.DeleteNetnsSymlink()`. So symlinks are removed. And now orphan sidecars get their symlinks removed too. Consistent.

Now potential issue: **double deletion in the deleteApplyNodes path** — line 612-616: DeleteContainer then DeleteNetnsSymlink. That's pre-existing (not changed in this PR — diff only touches line 730 area). Confirmed by diff: only line 730 added.

Now, is there a case where `deleteContainersDirect` is called for a container whose netns symlink should NOT be removed? E.g., in destroy-with-nodes path, orphan sidecars are containers with the lab label that are not in c.Nodes — they belong to this lab, so removing their symlink is correct.

What about `destroy --all` or destroy with multiple labs? Each CLab instance handles its own containers.

Now the more important check: **is the symlink removal needed at all for correctness of subsequent deploy?** Yes — LinkContainerNS does Lstat + Remove before Symlink, so stale symlinks are overwritten at deploy. But stale symlinks pointing at deleted netns paths cause `ip netns exec` failures and clutter. The bug fix is legitimate.

Potential edge: **name collision** — `DeleteNetnsSymlink` removes `/run/netns/<name>`. What if a user has an unrelated netns symlink with the same name (e.g., a plain `ip netns add foo` namespace that happens to collide with container name)? The node-based path already does exactly this via node.DeleteNetnsSymlink, so no new risk pattern.

Now, `_ =` error swallow: `DeleteNetnsSymlink` always returns nil anyway (it logs debug on error and returns nil). Consistent with line 616. Fine.

Another angle: **test quality**. The new Robot test steps check `test -L /run/netns/clab-mgmt13-n1`. These run with `Command Should Succeed` — does that keyword run with sudo? Let me check common.robot for the Command Should Succeed keyword. /run/netns requires root typically to test -L? `test -L` just does lstat — needs search permission on /run/netns; usually world-readable/executable. In CI, tests run as root typically. Let me check common.robot.
